You are merging partial summaries of the same RISC-V register derived from rule subsets.

Register: cycle

Partial summaries:
- The cycle CSR (address 0xC00) is an unprivileged read‑only shadow of the machine‑mode mcycle register that returns the current processor cycle count, with the RDCYCLE pseudoinstruction delivering the full XLEN‑wide value on XLEN ≥ 64 systems. The counter increments each CPU cycle unless inhibited by the CY bit in mcountinhibit or by the appropriate INH bits in mcyclecfg, and it continues counting irrespective of mcounteren settings. Reads are always permitted in M‑mode; in S‑ and U‑mode they are allowed only when the CY field of mcounteren (and, for U‑mode, also scounteren) is set, otherwise an illegal‑instruction exception is raised. A 64‑bit wrap generates exception code 13, and any write to the cycle CSR must be ignored and must not alter its value.

Remember, output must be ASCII-only text.
Return only the final paragraph.
============================================================
=========
RESPONSE:
=========
============================================================
The cycle CSR (address 0xC00) is an unprivileged read‑only shadow of the machine‑mode mcycle register that provides the current processor cycle count. The RDCYCLE pseudoinstruction returns the full XLEN‑wide value on implementations with XLEN ≥ 64. The counter increments each CPU cycle unless the CY bit in mcountinhibit or the corresponding INH bits in mcyclecfg are set, and it continues counting regardless of the contents of mcounteren. Reads are always permitted in M‑mode; in S‑mode they are permitted only if the CY field of mcounteren is set, and in U‑mode only if both that CY field and the CY field of scounteren are set, otherwise an illegal‑instruction exception is raised. A wrap of the 64‑bit value generates exception code 13, and any write to the cycle CSR is ignored and must not change its value.

You are merging partial summaries of the same RISC-V register derived from rule subsets.

Register: fcsr

Partial summaries:
- The fcsr is a per‑hart control‑status register that combines the floating‑point rounding‑mode field (frm) and the accrued‑exception‑flags field (fflags) used by floating‑point instructions. Both sub‑fields are readable and writable from any privilege level (URW), are invisible to other harts, and accesses never generate a trap regardless of which extensions are implemented. The rounding‑mode field supplies the dynamic rounding mode for operations that request it, while the flag field records any floating‑point exceptions that have occurred since software last cleared them; these flags do not cause traps. Modifications to the FS state do not affect the stored fcsr contents, and reserved bits are ignored or read as zero.

Remember, output must be ASCII-only text.
Return only the final paragraph.
============================================================
=========
RESPONSE:
=========
============================================================
The fcsr is a per‑hart control‑status register that contains the floating‑point rounding‑mode field (frm) and the accrued‑exception‑flags field (fflags). Both fields are readable and writable from any privilege level (URW), are hart‑local (invisible to other harts), and accesses never cause a trap irrespective of which floating‑point extensions are implemented. The frm field supplies the dynamic rounding mode for floating‑point operations that request a rounding mode, while the fflags field records any floating‑point exceptions that have occurred since software last cleared them; these flags do not generate traps. Changes to the floating‑point state (FS) do not modify the stored fcsr contents, and any reserved bits in the register are ignored or read as zero.

NAME: fflags
============================================================

PROMPT:

You are merging partial summaries of the same RISC-V register derived from rule subsets.

Register: fflags

Partial summaries:
- The fflags CSR records the five accrued floating‑point exception flags (Invalid, Divide‑by‑Zero, Overflow, Underflow, Inexact) that are set by any FP arithmetic instruction that encounters the corresponding condition and remain set until software clears them. It is part of the fcsr register but can be accessed directly with unprivileged read/write permissions, and reads or writes in any privilege mode generate no trap. The FRFLAGS instruction copies the current flag bits into an integer register, while FSFLAGS can read the flags and write a new value using the low bits of a source register, with writes updating only the five flag bits. Flag writes never cause traps, and writes larger than five bits are masked to the flag field; changing the FS field or disabling FP does not alter the stored flags.

Produce a single cohesive summary (4-6 sentences, one paragraph) that preserves all semantics present in the partial summaries, including fields, access control, privilege/virtualization constraints, and exception behavior.

Remember, output must be ASCII-only text.
Return only the final paragraph.

=========
RESPONSE:
=========
============================================================
The fflags CSR holds the five accrued floating‑point exception flags—Invalid, Divide‑by‑Zero, Overflow, Underflow, and Inexact—that are set by any floating‑point arithmetic instruction that encounters the corresponding condition and remain set until cleared by software. Although fflags is a subfield of the fcsr register, it is directly readable and writable with unprivileged access, and reads or writes to it in any privilege mode generate no trap. The FRFLAGS instruction copies the current flag bits into an integer register, while the FSFLAGS instruction reads the flags and writes a new value from the low five bits of a source register, updating only the flag field. Writes to fflags never cause traps; any bits above the low five are ignored (masked) and only the flag bits are affected. Changing the FS field of fcsr or disabling the floating‑point unit does not alter the stored fflags value.

It is accessed through the fcsr CSR (bits 7‑5) and by the FRRM and FSRM instructions, which transfer the 3‑bit value between integer registers and frm. The register is user‑read/write (URW) and can be read or written without exception in U‑, S‑, or M‑mode, even when the F, V, or related extensions are enabled. Writes are masked to the low three bits, so writing larger values does not set illegal patterns in the stored field.

RESPONSE:

The frm field is a 3‑bit sub‑field of the floating‑point control‑and‑status register (fcsr) located in bits 7‑5 that holds the current dynamic floating‑point rounding mode, selecting one of RNE, RTZ, RDN, RUP, or RMM. Encodings 101, 110, and 111 are not defined as rounding modes and cause an illegal‑instruction exception if they are interpreted as such. The value can be read or written via the FRRM and FSRM instructions, which transfer the 3‑bit field between an integer register and frm, and the register is classified as URW, permitting access in U‑, S‑, or M‑mode regardless of whether the F, V, or other extensions are implemented. Writes to frm are masked to the low three bits, so any larger value written is truncated and cannot set an illegal encoding in the stored field.

The hedeleg CSR is a 64‑bit hypervisor‑read/write register that selects which traps are delegated from HS‑mode to lower privilege levels. ============================================================

PROMPT:

You are merging partial summaries of the same RISC-V register derived from rule subsets.

Register: hip

Partial summaries:
- The hip CSR holds the set of pending hypervisor‑mode interrupts and is defined with HRW (read/write) access. Writable bits mirror the corresponding hvip bits and include virtual‑supervisor‑software (VSSIP), virtual‑supervisor‑timer (VSTIP), SGEIP and other delegated interrupt‑pending bits, while any bit not delegated (mideleg = 0) or set in sie must read as zero and bits in sip and hip are mutually exclusive. A pending interrupt can be cleared by writing a zero to its writable hip bit; if the bit is read‑only, it must be cleared via the hvip alias or another architectural mechanism, and all non‑writable bits read back as zero. Accesses to hip cause no exception in M‑ or HS‑mode (independent of extensions) but raise an illegal‑instruction exception in U‑mode.

Task:
Produce a single cohesive summary (4-6 sentences, one paragraph) that preserves all semantics present in the partial summaries, including fields, access control, privilege/virtualization constraints, and exception behavior.
- Do NOT introduce new semantics not present in the partial summaries.
- Do NOT remove constraints, field conditions, or exception cases.
- Remove redundancy by merging equivalent statements only.
- Prefer canonical RISC-V wording and precise terminology.
Remember, output must be ASCII-only text.
Return only the final paragraph.
============================================================
=========
RESPONSE:
=========
============================================================
The hip CSR contains the set of pending hypervisor‑mode interrupts and is defined with HRW (hypervisor‑read/write) access. Each writable bit in hip mirrors the corresponding bit in hvip and includes the virtual‑supervisor‑software interrupt (VSSIP), virtual‑supervisor‑timer interrupt (VSTIP), SGEIP, and any other interrupt‑pending bits that have been delegated to hypervisor mode (mideleg = 1). Bits that are not delegated (mideleg = 0) or that are set in the supervisor‑mode interrupt‑enable register sie read as zero, and the hip and sip registers are mutually exclusive for any given interrupt. A pending interrupt may be cleared by writing a zero to its writable hip bit; if the bit is read‑only it must be cleared via the hvip alias or by the architectural mechanism that governs that interrupt, and all non‑writable bits always read back as zero. Accesses to hip generate no exception in machine (M) or hypervisor‑supervisor (HS) mode, regardless of the presence of extensions, but raise an illegal‑instruction exception when executed in user (U) mode.

The R, W, and X bits control which memory operations are permitted for the address range selected by the A field, with the combination R=0 and W=1 reserved, and locked or Shared‑Region entries bypass normal permission checks for allowed accesses while causing load, store, or instruction‑access‑fault exceptions for prohibited accesses. Writes that would create executable entries forming M‑mode‑only or locked Shared‑Region rules are ignored unless mseccfg.RLB is set, and any CSR access that violates the above constraints generates the appropriate exception.

The upper 32 bits are permanently zero, there is no high‑half CSR, and accesses remain exception‑free in M‑mode when the Smstateen or Ssstateen extensions are implemented.

When a trap occurs, vstart records the faulting element index, and precise‑trap semantics require that only elements with index ≥ vstart may be modified, matching the effect of restarting the instruction at that index.

The vstart CSR (address 0x008) is a URW register that holds the architectural index of the vector element at which a trapped vector instruction should resume. It is automatically cleared to zero at the start of every vector instruction, including vsetvl, and hardware updates it on a trap; software may read or write it, with writes masked to the implemented field width. The register is accessible in M‑, S‑ and U‑mode unless the VS field is disabled, in which case any access generates an illegal‑instruction exception.
